From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is that I’ve been getting really jumpy, like way more than normal. I work around people coming and going all day, and every time a door slams or somebody drags one of those rolling suitcases quickly across the lobby, I just… I react before I can even think. My whole body jumps like something is happening again, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal, but my heart is pounding and it takes a minute to settle back down.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know it’s just a door or luggage or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to know that. That’s what pushed me to make the appointment. It’s been hard to just get through my shift without feeling on edge from those little sudden noises.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumpy reactions seem to be triggered by anything specific that reminds you of a past experience?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s mostly sudden loud sounds in the lobby that do it. Like if a door slams behind me, or somebody comes in fast and those suitcase wheels are rattling across the floor, my body just reacts before I even figure out what the sound is.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there thinking about it on purpose, but for a second it feels like it’s happening again, whatever my body thinks “it” is, and I just jump. Then I’m trying to play it off because I’m at the desk and people can see me, but my heart is racing and it takes me a minute to get it back down. It’s frustrating because my brain knows it’s just a door or luggage, but my body doesn’t seem to catch up right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant from your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been, actually. I didn’t really think of it as “avoiding” at first, because I’m still going to work and everything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can.\n\nThere’s this spot in the lobby where it happened, and if I have to walk right past it, I can feel my chest tighten up almost immediately. It’s like my body is telling me to get out of there before I’ve even really thought it through. So if there’s any excuse to go around the other way, even if it takes longer or looks kind of unnecessary, I do it.\n\nI know it probably seems small, just walking through a lobby, but when I get near that spot I just get this strong feeling like I need to be somewhere else. So, yeah, I’ve definitely been avoiding that area.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories or images from a past event that feel like they're happening all over again, even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that does happen. Not all the time, but it comes kind of out of nowhere, and that’s part of what bothers me about it. I can be doing something completely normal, like just answering the phone at the desk, and suddenly I’ll get this really clear picture in my head of that guest leaning over the counter and knocking the equipment toward me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to remember it or sitting there replaying it on purpose. I’ll just be mid-sentence on a normal call, trying to sound polite and professional, and then that image sort of flashes in—him coming over the counter area, the equipment shifting or coming toward me—and for a second it feels like I’m right back in that moment instead of just standing there doing my job.\n\nI usually keep talking because I don’t want the person on the phone to notice anything, but inside I’m kind of thrown off. It’s strange because it’s not even always triggered by something obvious. It just pops up, and then I have to remind myself, like, okay, I’m here, I’m just answering a call, nothing is happening right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened when those memories or images come up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to go there in my head, if that makes sense. When the image comes up, I kind of immediately want to shut it down or get away from whatever is making me think about it.\n\nAnd it’s not just in my head, either. I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, because walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened just… it makes my chest tighten up right away. Even before I’m fully thinking about it, I get this feeling like I need to get away from there. So I’ll go the longer way around, or I’ll find some excuse to avoid that part of the lobby if I can.\n\nI know it’s just a spot in the building, and I know nothing is happening right then, but when I pass it, it’s like everything in me wants to not look at it, not think about it, not be near it. So, yeah, I guess I am pushing it away and avoiding it more than I realized.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having negative thoughts about yourself or others that you just can't seem to shake off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a way I could clearly point to. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel embarrassed or frustrated with myself, like wondering if I should be handling all of this better, but I don’t know that I’d call it a constant negative thought about myself.\n\nAnd with other people… I don’t think I’m walking around thinking everyone is bad or anything like that. I’m more just uneasy in certain moments, if that makes sense. But I haven’t noticed some big belief stuck in my head like “I’m terrible” or “people can’t be trusted” all the time.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that as much as the other stuff.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these jumpy reactions and memories?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now. I mean, pretty much since that day when the guest threatened me at the front desk. Before that, I don’t remember being like this—at least not with this same jumpiness or having the memory pop back in like that.\n\nIt feels recent, but at the same time it’s been long enough that I’m starting to notice it as a pattern, if that makes sense. Like the first couple days I thought, okay, I’m just shaken up, that makes sense. But now it’s been around two weeks, and it’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when these jumpy reactions and memories happen, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’d say around a 6? Sometimes a 7, depending on how sudden it is.\n\nLike, the thing that really pushed me to come in is that every time a door slams or someone rolls a suitcase fast across the lobby, I jump like it’s happening again. It’s not just a little startled, either — my whole body kind of reacts before I can think. My heart starts pounding, and then it takes me a minute to get it back down and remind myself I’m just at work, nothing is actually happening in that second.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because other people probably just hear a normal lobby noise, but for me it feels like my body goes straight into alarm mode. So it’s not the worst feeling I’ve ever had, but it’s distressing enough that I’ve been worrying about it and noticing it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because of these experiences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though maybe not relationships exactly. I haven’t cut anyone off or anything like that. But I’ve definitely been avoiding certain parts of the hotel, and that kind of affects how I interact with people at work.\n\nThe main thing is I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can. Like, if I have to go somewhere and the normal way would take me past that spot in the lobby where it happened, I’ll go around instead. It’s not even always a conscious plan at first — I just find myself choosing the longer route because walking past that exact area makes my chest tighten, and I get this strong feeling like I need to get away from it.\n\nIt sounds kind of silly saying it out loud because it’s just a place in the lobby, and obviously it’s not happening again every time I walk by. But my body reacts like it is, or like it might. So I’ve been trying to avoid that spot as much as possible. As far as social activities or relationships, not really directly, but I guess if something means being near that area or standing around there talking, I’m more likely to excuse myself or take another route.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking or thinking about the incident with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’m avoiding thinking about it on purpose, exactly, but I’m definitely avoiding reminders of it. The clearest thing is that spot in the lobby where it happened. I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, even if it adds extra time or looks a little strange.\n\nIf I walk past that area, my chest tightens up almost right away, and I get this feeling like I just need to get away from it. So instead of dealing with that, I go around. It’s not like I’m making some big dramatic decision every time — it’s more like I notice myself doing it after the fact. Like, “Oh, I took the long way again.”\n\nTalking about it… I guess I don’t love talking about it, obviously. But the bigger thing is physically being near where it happened. That’s what I’ve been avoiding the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since this all started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been tracking my sleep closely, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I can say I’ve been more tense overall, but in terms of actual sleeping — like whether I’m up all night or not sleeping at all — I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern. Some nights I might lie there thinking for a bit, but I also did that sometimes before if work was stressful, so I don’t want to make it sound like it’s definitely because of this.\n\nSo… maybe a little disrupted here and there, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a major thing or something I’ve clearly connected to what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been sitting there thinking, like, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore,” or anything that clear. My routine’s been a little off, but I think that’s more just because I’m kind of preoccupied and trying to get through work and then go home. If something comes up that I normally like, I don’t think I feel totally uninterested in it.\n\nI guess I’d say I maybe haven’t been making as much effort, but it’s hard to separate that from just being tired or distracted. So, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things I used to enjoy. Not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Guilty or ashamed… I don’t know. So I’ll go the longer way around, or I’ll find some excuse to avoid that part of the lobby if I can.\n\nI know it’s just a spot in the building, and I know nothing is happening right then, but when I pass it, it’s like everything in me wants to not look at it, not think about it, not be near it. So, yeah, I guess I am pushing it away and avoiding it more than I realized.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having negative thoughts about yourself or others that you just can't seem to shake off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a way I could clearly point to. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel embarrassed or frustrated with myself, like wondering if I should be handling all of this better, but I don’t know that I’d call it a constant negative thought about myself.\n\nAnd with other people… I don’t think I’m walking around thinking everyone is bad or anything like that. I’m more just uneasy in certain moments, if that makes sense. But I haven’t noticed some big belief stuck in my head like “I’m terrible” or “people can’t be trusted” all the time.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that as much as the other stuff.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these jumpy reactions and memories?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now. I mean, pretty much since that day when the guest threatened me at the front desk. Before that, I don’t remember being like this—at least not with this same jumpiness or having the memory pop back in like that.\n\nIt feels recent, but at the same time it’s been long enough that I’m starting to notice it as a pattern, if that makes sense. Like the first couple days I thought, okay, I’m just shaken up, that makes sense. But now it’s been around two weeks, and it’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when these jumpy reactions and memories happen, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’d say around a 6? Sometimes a 7, depending on how sudden it is.\n\nLike, the thing that really pushed me to come in is that every time a door slams or someone rolls a suitcase fast across the lobby, I jump like it’s happening again. It’s not just a little startled, either — my whole body kind of reacts before I can think. My heart starts pounding, and then it takes me a minute to get it back down and remind myself I’m just at work, nothing is actually happening in that second.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because other people probably just hear a normal lobby noise, but for me it feels like my body goes straight into alarm mode. So it’s not the worst feeling I’ve ever had, but it’s distressing enough that I’ve been worrying about it and noticing it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because of these experiences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though maybe not relationships exactly. I haven’t cut anyone off or anything like that. But I’ve definitely been avoiding certain parts of the hotel, and that kind of affects how I interact with people at work.\n\nThe main thing is I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can. Like, if I have to go somewhere and the normal way would take me past that spot in the lobby where it happened, I’ll go around instead. It’s not even always a conscious plan at first — I just find myself choosing the longer route because walking past that exact area makes my chest tighten, and I get this strong feeling like I need to get away from it.\n\nIt sounds kind of silly saying it out loud because it’s just a place in the lobby, and obviously it’s not happening again every time I walk by. But my body reacts like it is, or like it might. So I’ve been trying to avoid that spot as much as possible. As far as social activities or relationships, not really directly, but I guess if something means being near that area or standing around there talking, I’m more likely to excuse myself or take another route.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking or thinking about the incident with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’m avoiding thinking about it on purpose, exactly, but I’m definitely avoiding reminders of it. The clearest thing is that spot in the lobby where it happened. I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, even if it adds extra time or looks a little strange.\n\nIf I walk past that area, my chest tightens up almost right away, and I get this feeling like I just need to get away from it. So instead of dealing with that, I go around. It’s not like I’m making some big dramatic decision every time — it’s more like I notice myself doing it after the fact. Like, “Oh, I took the long way again.”\n\nTalking about it… I guess I don’t love talking about it, obviously. But the bigger thing is physically being near where it happened. That’s what I’ve been avoiding the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since this all started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been tracking my sleep closely, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I can say I’ve been more tense overall, but in terms of actual sleeping — like whether I’m up all night or not sleeping at all — I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern. Some nights I might lie there thinking for a bit, but I also did that sometimes before if work was stressful, so I don’t want to make it sound like it’s definitely because of this.\n\nSo… maybe a little disrupted here and there, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a major thing or something I’ve clearly connected to what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been sitting there thinking, like, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore,” or anything that clear. My routine’s been a little off, but I think that’s more just because I’m kind of preoccupied and trying to get through work and then go home. If something comes up that I normally like, I don’t think I feel totally uninterested in it.\n\nI guess I’d say I maybe haven’t been making as much effort, but it’s hard to separate that from just being tired or distracted. So, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things I used to enjoy. Not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Guilty or ashamed… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been the main feeling, honestly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, like wondering if I should’ve handled it differently or said something else, but it doesn’t really feel like guilt exactly. More like second-guessing, if that makes sense. Like when something bad happens at work and afterward you replay it and think, “Could I have done something smoother?” But I don’t think I’m walking around feeling ashamed of myself.\n\nI guess there’s maybe some embarrassment just because it happened at the front desk, where people can see things, and I don’t like feeling like I wasn’t fully in control of the situation. But guilt… no, not really. I don’t feel like I caused it or that it was my fault in some big way. It’s more that I wish it hadn’t happened, and I wish I could stop mentally picking at little pieces of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly, no.\n\nLike, I don’t think I’m walking around scanning every room or expecting something bad to happen all the time. So… yeah, roughly two weeks now, ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life or relationships because of these jumpy reactions and memories?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I know I’m doing it, and it’s frustrating because it’s not really like me.\n\nMy manager noticed too, I guess. He shortened a couple of my shifts recently, not in a punitive way exactly, more like, “Let’s ease you back into it,” but still… it made me feel kind of embarrassed. I’m showing up and trying to do my job, but I’m not as quick or as available to guests in the lobby as I normally would be. I haven’t noticed that as much as the other stuff.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these jumpy reactions and memories?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now. I mean, pretty much since that day when the guest threatened me at the front desk. Before that, I don’t remember being like this—at least not with this same jumpiness or having the memory pop back in like that.\n\nIt feels recent, but at the same time it’s been long enough that I’m starting to notice it as a pattern, if that makes sense. Like the first couple days I thought, okay, I’m just shaken up, that makes sense. But now it’s been around two weeks, and it’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when these jumpy reactions and memories happen, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’d say around a 6? Sometimes a 7, depending on how sudden it is.\n\nLike, the thing that really pushed me to come in is that every time a door slams or someone rolls a suitcase fast across the lobby, I jump like it’s happening again. It’s not just a little startled, either — my whole body kind of reacts before I can think. My heart starts pounding, and then it takes me a minute to get it back down and remind myself I’m just at work, nothing is actually happening in that second.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because other people probably just hear a normal lobby noise, but for me it feels like my body goes straight into alarm mode. So it’s not the worst feeling I’ve ever had, but it’s distressing enough that I’ve been worrying about it and noticing it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because of these experiences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though maybe not relationships exactly. I haven’t cut anyone off or anything like that. But I’ve definitely been avoiding certain parts of the hotel, and that kind of affects how I interact with people at work.\n\nThe main thing is I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can. Like, if I have to go somewhere and the normal way would take me past that spot in the lobby where it happened, I’ll go around instead. It’s not even always a conscious plan at first — I just find myself choosing the longer route because walking past that exact area makes my chest tighten, and I get this strong feeling like I need to get away from it.\n\nIt sounds kind of silly saying it out loud because it’s just a place in the lobby, and obviously it’s not happening again every time I walk by. But my body reacts like it is, or like it might. So I’ve been trying to avoid that spot as much as possible. As far as social activities or relationships, not really directly, but I guess if something means being near that area or standing around there talking, I’m more likely to excuse myself or take another route.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking or thinking about the incident with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’m avoiding thinking about it on purpose, exactly, but I’m definitely avoiding reminders of it. The clearest thing is that spot in the lobby where it happened. I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, even if it adds extra time or looks a little strange.\n\nIf I walk past that area, my chest tightens up almost right away, and I get this feeling like I just need to get away from it. So instead of dealing with that, I go around. It’s not like I’m making some big dramatic decision every time — it’s more like I notice myself doing it after the fact. Like, “Oh, I took the long way again.”\n\nTalking about it… I guess I don’t love talking about it, obviously. But the bigger thing is physically being near where it happened. That’s what I’ve been avoiding the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since this all started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been tracking my sleep closely, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I can say I’ve been more tense overall, but in terms of actual sleeping — like whether I’m up all night or not sleeping at all — I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern. Some nights I might lie there thinking for a bit, but I also did that sometimes before if work was stressful, so I don’t want to make it sound like it’s definitely because of this.\n\nSo… maybe a little disrupted here and there, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a major thing or something I’ve clearly connected to what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been sitting there thinking, like, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore,” or anything that clear. My routine’s been a little off, but I think that’s more just because I’m kind of preoccupied and trying to get through work and then go home. If something comes up that I normally like, I don’t think I feel totally uninterested in it.\n\nI guess I’d say I maybe haven’t been making as much effort, but it’s hard to separate that from just being tired or distracted. So, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things I used to enjoy. Not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Guilty or ashamed… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been the main feeling, honestly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, like wondering if I should’ve handled it differently or said something else, but it doesn’t really feel like guilt exactly. More like second-guessing, if that makes sense. Like when something bad happens at work and afterward you replay it and think, “Could I have done something smoother?” But I don’t think I’m walking around feeling ashamed of myself.\n\nI guess there’s maybe some embarrassment just because it happened at the front desk, where people can see things, and I don’t like feeling like I wasn’t fully in control of the situation. But guilt… no, not really. I don’t feel like I caused it or that it was my fault in some big way. It’s more that I wish it hadn’t happened, and I wish I could stop mentally picking at little pieces of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly, no.\n\nLike, I don’t think I’m walking around scanning every room or expecting something bad to happen all the time. I do notice myself being a little more aware at work sometimes, especially if someone’s voice gets loud or there’s some kind of commotion near the desk, but I don’t know if that’s “on the lookout for danger” exactly, or just… being alert because of where I work.\n\nOutside of that, like at the grocery store or driving or being at home, I don’t think I’m actively looking for threats. I’m not checking exits or watching everyone around me or anything like that. Maybe I’m a little more tense than usual in certain moments, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling like danger is everywhere.\n\nSo… no, not really. Not in a constant way.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding that spot in the lobby where the incident occurred?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks, I think. Basically since that guest threatened me at the front desk.\n\nI didn’t really make a decision like, “I’m not going to stand there anymore,” at least not at first. It was more that the next time I was in the lobby, I found myself kind of drifting to the other side or finding a reason to stand somewhere else. And then I noticed I kept doing it. So… yeah, roughly two weeks now, ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life or relationships because of these jumpy reactions and memories?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. But it has been affecting things.\n\nAt the reservation desk, I’ve been slower than usual. Stuff that used to be pretty automatic—checking something, answering a question, helping someone with a booking—I catch myself hesitating or losing my rhythm. And if something means I have to go across the lobby, especially over near that spot, I’ll find some reason to delay it or ask someone else, or I’ll do another task first. I know I’m doing it, and it’s frustrating because it’s not really like me.\n\nMy manager noticed too, I guess. He shortened a couple of my shifts recently, not in a punitive way exactly, more like, “Let’s ease you back into it,” but still… it made me feel kind of embarrassed. I’m showing up and trying to do my job, but I’m not as quick or as available to guests in the lobby as I normally would be. So yes, I’d say it’s getting in the way some, mostly at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy doing since the incident happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been doing a ton of fun stuff lately, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest in it. It’s more like… with work being weird and me feeling a little off afterward, I’ve been more likely to just go home and keep things low-key. But if a friend invited me to watch a game or grab food or something, I don’t think I’d feel like, “No, I don’t care about that anymore.”\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that my interests have gone away. I still like the same things. I may just be less motivated some evenings, or more tired, but I don’t think it’s a loss of interest exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I’d say I’m participating less in a major way. Like, I haven’t stopped doing things on purpose, or decided I don’t want to see people or do anything I normally like.

I didn’t really think of it as “avoiding” at first, because I’m still going to work and everything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can.\n\nThere’s this spot in the lobby where it happened, and if I have to walk right past it, I can feel my chest tighten up almost immediately. It’s like my body is telling me to get out of there before I’ve even really thought it through. So if there’s any excuse to go around the other way, even if it takes longer or looks kind of unnecessary, I do it.\n\nI know it probably seems small, just walking through a lobby, but when I get near that spot I just get this strong feeling like I need to be somewhere else. So, yeah, I’ve definitely been avoiding that area.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories or images from a past event that feel like they're happening all over again, even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that does happen. Not all the time, but it comes kind of out of nowhere, and that’s part of what bothers me about it. I can be doing something completely normal, like just answering the phone at the desk, and suddenly I’ll get this really clear picture in my head of that guest leaning over the counter and knocking the equipment toward me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to remember it or sitting there replaying it on purpose. I’ll just be mid-sentence on a normal call, trying to sound polite and professional, and then that image sort of flashes in—him coming over the counter area, the equipment shifting or coming toward me—and for a second it feels like I’m right back in that moment instead of just standing there doing my job.\n\nI usually keep talking because I don’t want the person on the phone to notice anything, but inside I’m kind of thrown off. It’s strange because it’s not even always triggered by something obvious. It just pops up, and then I have to remind myself, like, okay, I’m here, I’m just answering a call, nothing is happening right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened when those memories or images come up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to go there in my head, if that makes sense. When the image comes up, I kind of immediately want to shut it down or get away from whatever is making me think about it.\n\nAnd it’s not just in my head, either. I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, because walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened just… it makes my chest tighten up right away. Even before I’m fully thinking about it, I get this feeling like I need to get away from there. So I’ll go the longer way around, or I’ll find some excuse to avoid that part of the lobby if I can.\n\nI know it’s just a spot in the building, and I know nothing is happening right then, but when I pass it, it’s like everything in me wants to not look at it, not think about it, not be near it. So, yeah, I guess I am pushing it away and avoiding it more than I realized.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having negative thoughts about yourself or others that you just can't seem to shake off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a way I could clearly point to. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel embarrassed or frustrated with myself, like wondering if I should be handling all of this better, but I don’t know that I’d call it a constant negative thought about myself.\n\nAnd with other people… I don’t think I’m walking around thinking everyone is bad or anything like that. I’m more just uneasy in certain moments, if that makes sense. But I haven’t noticed some big belief stuck in my head like “I’m terrible” or “people can’t be trusted” all the time.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that as much as the other stuff.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these jumpy reactions and memories?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now. I mean, pretty much since that day when the guest threatened me at the front desk. Before that, I don’t remember being like this—at least not with this same jumpiness or having the memory pop back in like that.\n\nIt feels recent, but at the same time it’s been long enough that I’m starting to notice it as a pattern, if that makes sense. Like the first couple days I thought, okay, I’m just shaken up, that makes sense. But now it’s been around two weeks, and it’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when these jumpy reactions and memories happen, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’d say around a 6? Sometimes a 7, depending on how sudden it is.\n\nLike, the thing that really pushed me to come in is that every time a door slams or someone rolls a suitcase fast across the lobby, I jump like it’s happening again. It’s not just a little startled, either — my whole body kind of reacts before I can think. My heart starts pounding, and then it takes me a minute to get it back down and remind myself I’m just at work, nothing is actually happening in that second.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because other people probably just hear a normal lobby noise, but for me it feels like my body goes straight into alarm mode. So it’s not the worst feeling I’ve ever had, but it’s distressing enough that I’ve been worrying about it and noticing it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because of these experiences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though maybe not relationships exactly. I haven’t cut anyone off or anything like that. But I’ve definitely been avoiding certain parts of the hotel, and that kind of affects how I interact with people at work.\n\nThe main thing is I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can. Like, if I have to go somewhere and the normal way would take me past that spot in the lobby where it happened, I’ll go around instead. It’s not even always a conscious plan at first — I just find myself choosing the longer route because walking past that exact area makes my chest tighten, and I get this strong feeling like I need to get away from it.\n\nIt sounds kind of silly saying it out loud because it’s just a place in the lobby, and obviously it’s not happening again every time I walk by. But my body reacts like it is, or like it might. So I’ve been trying to avoid that spot as much as possible. As far as social activities or relationships, not really directly, but I guess if something means being near that area or standing around there talking, I’m more likely to excuse myself or take another route.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking or thinking about the incident with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’m avoiding thinking about it on purpose, exactly, but I’m definitely avoiding reminders of it. The clearest thing is that spot in the lobby where it happened. I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, even if it adds extra time or looks a little strange.\n\nIf I walk past that area, my chest tightens up almost right away, and I get this feeling like I just need to get away from it. So instead of dealing with that, I go around. It’s not like I’m making some big dramatic decision every time — it’s more like I notice myself doing it after the fact. Like, “Oh, I took the long way again.”\n\nTalking about it… I guess I don’t love talking about it, obviously. But the bigger thing is physically being near where it happened. That’s what I’ve been avoiding the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since this all started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been tracking my sleep closely, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I can say I’ve been more tense overall, but in terms of actual sleeping — like whether I’m up all night or not sleeping at all — I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern. Some nights I might lie there thinking for a bit, but I also did that sometimes before if work was stressful, so I don’t want to make it sound like it’s definitely because of this.\n\nSo… maybe a little disrupted here and there, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a major thing or something I’ve clearly connected to what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been sitting there thinking, like, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore,” or anything that clear. My routine’s been a little off, but I think that’s more just because I’m kind of preoccupied and trying to get through work and then go home. If something comes up that I normally like, I don’t think I feel totally uninterested in it.\n\nI guess I’d say I maybe haven’t been making as much effort, but it’s hard to separate that from just being tired or distracted. So, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things I used to enjoy. Not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Guilty or ashamed… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been the main feeling, honestly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, like wondering if I should’ve handled it differently or said something else, but it doesn’t really feel like guilt exactly. More like second-guessing, if that makes sense. Like when something bad happens at work and afterward you replay it and think, “Could I have done something smoother?” But I don’t think I’m walking around feeling ashamed of myself.\n\nI guess there’s maybe some embarrassment just because it happened at the front desk, where people can see things, and I don’t like feeling like I wasn’t fully in control of the situation. But guilt… no, not really. I don’t feel like I caused it or that it was my fault in some big way. It’s more that I wish it hadn’t happened, and I wish I could stop mentally picking at little pieces of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly, no.\n\nLike, I don’t think I’m walking around scanning every room or expecting something bad to happen all the time. I do notice myself being a little more aware at work sometimes, especially if someone’s voice gets loud or there’s some kind of commotion near the desk, but I don’t know if that’s “on the lookout for danger” exactly, or just… being alert because of where I work.\n\nOutside of that, like at the grocery store or driving or being at home, I don’t think I’m actively looking for threats. I’m not checking exits or watching everyone around me or anything like that. Maybe I’m a little more tense than usual in certain moments, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling like danger is everywhere.\n\nSo… no, not really. Not in a constant way.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding that spot in the lobby where the incident occurred?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks, I think. Basically since that guest threatened me at the front desk.\n\nI didn’t really make a decision like, “I’m not going to stand there anymore,” at least not at first. It was more that the next time I was in the lobby, I found myself kind of drifting to the other side or finding a reason to stand somewhere else. And then I noticed I kept doing it. So… yeah, roughly two weeks now, ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life or relationships because of these jumpy reactions and memories?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. But it has been affecting things.\n\nAt the reservation desk, I’ve been slower than usual. Stuff that used to be pretty automatic—checking something, answering a question, helping someone with a booking—I catch myself hesitating or losing my rhythm. And if something means I have to go across the lobby, especially over near that spot, I’ll find some reason to delay it or ask someone else, or I’ll do another task first. I know I’m doing it, and it’s frustrating because it’s not really like me.\n\nMy manager noticed too, I guess. He shortened a couple of my shifts recently, not in a punitive way exactly, more like, “Let’s ease you back into it,” but still… it made me feel kind of embarrassed. I’m showing up and trying to do my job, but I’m not as quick or as available to guests in the lobby as I normally would be. So yes, I’d say it’s getting in the way some, mostly at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy doing since the incident happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I haven’t exactly been doing a ton of fun stuff lately, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest in it. It’s more like… with work being weird and me feeling a little off afterward, I’ve been more likely to just go home and keep things low-key. But if a friend invited me to watch a game or grab food or something, I don’t think I’d feel like, “No, I don’t care about that anymore.”\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that my interests have gone away. I still like the same things. I may just be less motivated some evenings, or more tired, but I don’t think it’s a loss of interest exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself participating less in activities that you normally find enjoyable or fulfilling since the incident occurred?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m participating less in a major way. Like, I haven’t stopped doing things on purpose, or decided I don’t want to see people or do anything I normally like.\n\nIt’s more that the last couple weeks have felt a little… off, I guess. So if I had a choice between going out and doing something or just going home and keeping it quiet, I might lean more toward staying in. But I don’t know if that’s because I’m not enjoying things anymore. I don’t think it is. It feels more like I’m just tired or distracted after work.\n\nSo, overall, no, I haven’t really noticed a clear drop in doing enjoyable things. Maybe a little less spontaneous, if that makes sense, but not like I’ve stopped caring about them."
